It has completely tanked my productivity at work, and I've missed critical family events and stopped going to the salsa dancing and social meetups I used to love.\n\nDoctor: Have these checking rituals and intrusive thoughts been present for at least 2 weeks, and do you feel they are excessive or unreasonable even while you can’t stop them?\n\nPatient: Yes, this has been building up for way longer than two weeks—it actually started about two years ago after my promotion at work. I absolutely know that these thoughts and checking habits are completely irrational and unreasonable, but despite knowing that, I still feel totally powerless to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: When you try to resist the checking or mental reviewing, do you feel intense anxiety or distress that only goes down after you perform the ritual?\n\nPatient: Yes, resisting it is almost impossible because the anxiety becomes absolutely crushing, and I get this terrifying feeling that something catastrophic is about to happen. The only way that intense panic finally fades is if I give in and complete the checking ritual until it feels \"just right.\""

When his company underwent a major reorganization two years ago, he was promoted to oversee a larger IT infrastructure portfolio, including cybersecurity for several high-profile clients, which dramatically increased his sense of responsibility and the potential consequences of mistakes. Over the following months, he started staying later and later at the office and at home, repeatedly reviewing configurations and incident reports, and turning down salsa socials and tech meetups he used to enjoy because he felt compelled to keep checking and rechecking his work. The tipping point came when he missed a critical family celebration and then had a minor panic episode on the train home after realizing that, despite all his late nights, he still felt unable to trust that things were \"safe\"; a colleague who noticed his deteriorating sleep and performance urged him to see a psychiatrist, and Hector finally agreed when he recognized that his checking routines had become unmanageable and were crowding out nearly everything else in his life.\n- Chief Complaint: “I’m stuck in this loop of double- and triple-checking my work for hours, and I can’t move on even when I know it’s already done right.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S065 (Obsessions): Lately my brain keeps ambushing me with these vivid flashes of a server getting hacked because I missed one tiny setting, or my nephew getting hurt because I didn’t check a door lock, and even when I tell myself it’s irrational and I’ve already verified everything, the “what if you missed something” loop just won’t shut off and drowns out whatever else I’m trying to do.\n- S066 (Compulsions): When I finish a security change or even just lock my front door, I feel this crushing need to go back and reread every line of the config or jiggle the handle over and over, sometimes for an hour or more, because until I repeat the whole ritual just right I can’t shake the feeling that if I stop, something catastrophic will happen and it’ll be my fault.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: My checking has gotten so out of hand that I miss family events, avoid social things I used to love, and even at work my productivity has dropped because I burn hours re-reviewing tasks instead of moving projects forward.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m stuck in this loop of double- and triple-checking my work for hours, and I can’t move on even when I know it’s already done right. It has gotten to the point where it's just completely exhausting and I don't know how to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Besides the repeated checking, do you also have unwanted intrusive thoughts or images that keep coming back and make you feel driven to do other rituals, like counting, ordering, cleaning, or mental reviewing?\n\nPatient: Yes, my mind gets flooded with vivid flashes of a server getting hacked or my nephew getting hurt because I missed something. To stop those thoughts, I feel forced to reread code or jiggle door handles over and over until it feels \"just right\" to prevent a catastrophe. I don't do counting or cleaning, but this constant checking and mental reviewing is absolutely exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you have both the repeated checking/mental rituals and the intrusive scary thoughts, or is one of these much more dominant than the other?\n\nPatient: They are both incredibly prominent because they are completely locked together. The scary thoughts of a server hack or my family getting hurt act as the trigger, which then forces me into the physical checking and re-checking to try and quiet those thoughts.\n\nDoctor: About how much time each day do these checking and mental review rituals take, and how much do they interfere with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: These rituals eat up hours of my day, often taking an hour or more just to finish checking a single lock or config line. It has completely tanked my productivity at work, and I've missed critical family events and stopped going to the salsa dancing and social meetups I used to love.\n\nDoctor: Have these checking rituals and intrusive thoughts been present for at least 2 weeks, and do you feel they are excessive or unreasonable even while you can’t stop them?\n\nPatient: Yes, this has been building up for way longer than two weeks—it actually started about two years ago after my promotion at work. I absolutely know that these thoughts and checking habits are completely irrational and unreasonable, but despite knowing that, I still feel totally powerless to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: When you try to resist the checking or mental reviewing, do you feel intense anxiety or distress that only goes down after you perform the ritual?\n\nPatient: Yes, resisting it is almost impossible because the anxiety becomes absolutely crushing, and I get this terrifying feeling that something catastrophic is about to happen. The only way that intense panic finally fades is if I give in and complete the checking ritual until it feels \"just right.\"\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other repetitive behaviors or mental rituals besides checking, such as counting, ordering, repeating words silently, or needing things to feel exactly 'just right'?\n\nPatient: I don't do things like counting, ordering, or repeating words, but I absolutely need things to feel \"just right.\" When I'm checking a configuration or jiggling a door handle, I have to repeat the physical ritual until it feels exactly right, otherwise I'm overwhelmed by the feeling that something catastrophic will happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you recognize these thoughts and checking rituals as your own thoughts that feel unwanted or unreasonable, or do you ever believe they are actually true or that someone/something outside you is controlling them?"

I don't do counting or cleaning, but this constant checking and mental reviewing is absolutely exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you have both the repeated checking/mental rituals and the intrusive scary thoughts, or is one of these much more dominant than the other?\n\nPatient: They are both incredibly prominent because they are completely locked together. The scary thoughts of a server hack or my family getting hurt act as the trigger, which then forces me into the physical checking and re-checking to try and quiet those thoughts.\n\nDoctor: About how much time each day do these checking and mental review rituals take, and how much do they interfere with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: These rituals eat up hours of my day, often taking an hour or more just to finish checking a single lock or config line. It has completely tanked my productivity at work, and I've missed critical family events and stopped going to the salsa dancing and social meetups I used to love.\n\nDoctor: Have these checking rituals and intrusive thoughts been present for at least 2 weeks, and do you feel they are excessive or unreasonable even while you can’t stop them?\n\nPatient: Yes, this has been building up for way longer than two weeks—it actually started about two years ago after my promotion at work. I absolutely know that these thoughts and checking habits are completely irrational and unreasonable, but despite knowing that, I still feel totally powerless to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: When you try to resist the checking or mental reviewing, do you feel intense anxiety or distress that only goes down after you perform the ritual?\n\nPatient: Yes, resisting it is almost impossible because the anxiety becomes absolutely crushing, and I get this terrifying feeling that something catastrophic is about to happen. The only way that intense panic finally fades is if I give in and complete the checking ritual until it feels \"just right.\"\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other repetitive behaviors or mental rituals besides checking, such as counting, ordering, repeating words silently, or needing things to feel exactly 'just right'?\n\nPatient: I don't do things like counting, ordering, or repeating words, but I absolutely need things to feel \"just right.\" When I'm checking a configuration or jiggling a door handle, I have to repeat the physical ritual until it feels exactly right, otherwise I'm overwhelmed by the feeling that something catastrophic will happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you recognize these thoughts and checking rituals as your own thoughts that feel unwanted or unreasonable, or do you ever believe they are actually true or that someone/something outside you is controlling them?\n\nPatient: I absolutely recognize them as my own thoughts, and I know they are completely irrational and unreasonable. Nobody else is controlling my mind, and I don't believe these fears are literally true, but the anxiety is so intense that I still feel forced to do the rituals anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to do the checking even when you are not anxious or worried, or is it mainly to reduce the intrusive fear that something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: No, I never do it when I'm feeling calm or unbothered. It is entirely driven by that intense, intrusive fear that something catastrophic is going to happen and it will be my fault. I only do the checking to try and shake that crushing feeling of worry so I can get some relief."

The only way that intense panic finally fades is if I give in and complete the checking ritual until it feels \"just right.\"\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other repetitive behaviors or mental rituals besides checking, such as counting, ordering, repeating words silently, or needing things to feel exactly 'just right'?\n\nPatient: I don't do things like counting, ordering, or repeating words, but I absolutely need things to feel \"just right.\" When I'm checking a configuration or jiggling a door handle, I have to repeat the physical ritual until it feels exactly right, otherwise I'm overwhelmed by the feeling that something catastrophic will happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you recognize these thoughts and checking rituals as your own thoughts that feel unwanted or unreasonable, or do you ever believe they are actually true or that someone/something outside you is controlling them?\n\nPatient: I absolutely recognize them as my own thoughts, and I know they are completely irrational and unreasonable. Nobody else is controlling my mind, and I don't believe these fears are literally true, but the anxiety is so intense that I still feel forced to do the rituals anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to do the checking even when you are not anxious or worried, or is it mainly to reduce the intrusive fear that something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: No, I never do it when I'm feeling calm or unbothered. It is entirely driven by that intense, intrusive fear that something catastrophic is going to happen and it will be my fault. I only do the checking to try and shake that crushing feeling of worry so I can get some relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have any other obsessive-compulsive symptoms, such as contamination fears and washing, ordering/symmetry rituals, counting, or repeating words or prayers in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience any of those things like contamination fears, washing, counting, or repeating words. My struggles are strictly focused on my checking routines for work configurations and door locks to prevent something catastrophic from happening."
  }

Over the following months, he started staying later and later at the office and at home, repeatedly reviewing configurations and incident reports, and turning down salsa socials and tech meetups he used to enjoy because he felt compelled to keep checking and rechecking his work. The tipping point came when he missed a critical family celebration and then had a minor panic episode on the train home after realizing that, despite all his late nights, he still felt unable to trust that things were \"safe\"; a colleague who noticed his deteriorating sleep and performance urged him to see a psychiatrist, and Hector finally agreed when he recognized that his checking routines had become unmanageable and were crowding out nearly everything else in his life.\n- Chief Complaint: “I’m stuck in this loop of double- and triple-checking my work for hours, and I can’t move on even when I know it’s already done right.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S065 (Obsessions): Lately my brain keeps ambushing me with these vivid flashes of a server getting hacked because I missed one tiny setting, or my nephew getting hurt because I didn’t check a door lock, and even when I tell myself it’s irrational and I’ve already verified everything, the “what if you missed something” loop just won’t shut off and drowns out whatever else I’m trying to do.\n- S066 (Compulsions): When I finish a security change or even just lock my front door, I feel this crushing need to go back and reread every line of the config or jiggle the handle over and over, sometimes for an hour or more, because until I repeat the whole ritual just right I can’t shake the feeling that if I stop, something catastrophic will happen and it’ll be my fault.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: My checking has gotten so out of hand that I miss family events, avoid social things I used to love, and even at work my productivity has dropped because I burn hours re-reviewing tasks instead of moving projects forward.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint.     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m stuck in this loop of double- and triple-checking my work for hours, and I can’t move on even when I know it’s already done right. It has gotten to the point where it's just completely exhausting and I don't know how to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Besides the repeated checking, do you also have unwanted intrusive thoughts or images that keep coming back and make you feel driven to do other rituals, like counting, ordering, cleaning, or mental reviewing?\n\nPatient: Yes, my mind gets flooded with vivid flashes of a server getting hacked or my nephew getting hurt because I missed something. To stop those thoughts, I feel forced to reread code or jiggle door handles over and over until it feels \"just right\" to prevent a catastrophe. I don't do counting or cleaning, but this constant checking and mental reviewing is absolutely exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you have both the repeated checking/mental rituals and the intrusive scary thoughts, or is one of these much more dominant than the other?\n\nPatient: They are both incredibly prominent because they are completely locked together. The scary thoughts of a server hack or my family getting hurt act as the trigger, which then forces me into the physical checking and re-checking to try and quiet those thoughts.\n\nDoctor: About how much time each day do these checking and mental review rituals take, and how much do they interfere with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: These rituals eat up hours of my day, often taking an hour or more just to finish checking a single lock or config line. It has completely tanked my productivity at work, and I've missed critical family events and stopped going to the salsa dancing and social meetups I used to love.\n\nDoctor: Have these checking rituals and intrusive thoughts been present for at least 2 weeks, and do you feel they are excessive or unreasonable even while you can’t stop them?\n\nPatient: Yes, this has been building up for way longer than two weeks—it actually started about two years ago after my promotion at work. I absolutely know that these thoughts and checking habits are completely irrational and unreasonable, but despite knowing that, I still feel totally powerless to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: When you try to resist the checking or mental reviewing, do you feel intense anxiety or distress that only goes down after you perform the ritual?\n\nPatient: Yes, resisting it is almost impossible because the anxiety becomes absolutely crushing, and I get this terrifying feeling that something catastrophic is about to happen. The only way that intense panic finally fades is if I give in and complete the checking ritual until it feels \"just right.\"\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other repetitive behaviors or mental rituals besides checking, such as counting, ordering, repeating words silently, or needing things to feel exactly 'just right'?\n\nPatient: I don't do things like counting, ordering, or repeating words, but I absolutely need things to feel \"just right.\" When I'm checking a configuration or jiggling a door handle, I have to repeat the physical ritual until it feels exactly right, otherwise I'm overwhelmed by the feeling that something catastrophic will happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you recognize these thoughts and checking rituals as your own thoughts that feel unwanted or unreasonable, or do you ever believe they are actually true or that someone/something outside you is controlling them?\n\nPatient: I absolutely recognize them as my own thoughts, and I know they are completely irrational and unreasonable. Nobody else is controlling my mind, and I don't believe these fears are literally true, but the anxiety is so intense that I still feel forced to do the rituals anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to do the checking even when you are not anxious or worried, or is it mainly to reduce the intrusive fear that something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: No, I never do it when I'm feeling calm or unbothered. It is entirely driven by that intense, intrusive fear that something catastrophic is going to happen and it will be my fault. I only do the checking to try and shake that crushing feeling of worry so I can get some relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have any other obsessive-compulsive symptoms, such as contamination fears and washing, ordering/symmetry rituals, counting, or repeating words or prayers in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience any of those things like contamination fears, washing, counting, or repeating words. My struggles are strictly focused on my checking routines for work configurations and door locks to prevent something catastrophic from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do these checking rituals or intrusive fears ever feel like they are not really yours, or do you ever hear/feel a voice or outside force telling you to do them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't hear any voices or feel like an outside force is controlling me. Even though these thoughts and urges feel like they ambush me out of nowhere, I recognize them as coming entirely from my own mind. They are just incredibly intrusive and hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: When you try to resist the checking, do you feel a strong urge to repeat it until it feels exactly right or until the anxiety drops, even if you already know the task is done?"
  }

Yes, that is exactly what happens. If I try to resist, this crushing anxiety builds up and forces me to keep rereading the configuration lines or jiggling the door handle. I have to repeat it over and over until it feels "just right" and the dread subsides, even though intellectually I know it was already done correctly.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m stuck in this loop of double- and triple-checking my work for hours, and I can’t move on even when I know it’s already done right. It has gotten to the point where it's just completely exhausting and I don't know how to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Besides the repeated checking, do you also have unwanted intrusive thoughts or images that keep coming back and make you feel driven to do other rituals, like counting, ordering, cleaning, or mental reviewing?\n\nPatient: Yes, my mind gets flooded with vivid flashes of a server getting hacked or my nephew getting hurt because I missed something. To stop those thoughts, I feel forced to reread code or jiggle door handles over and over until it feels \"just right\" to prevent a catastrophe. I don't do counting or cleaning, but this constant checking and mental reviewing is absolutely exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you have both the repeated checking/mental rituals and the intrusive scary thoughts, or is one of these much more dominant than the other?\n\nPatient: They are both incredibly prominent because they are completely locked together. The scary thoughts of a server hack or my family getting hurt act as the trigger, which then forces me into the physical checking and re-checking to try and quiet those thoughts.\n\nDoctor: About how much time each day do these checking and mental review rituals take, and how much do they interfere with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: These rituals eat up hours of my day, often taking an hour or more just to finish checking a single lock or config line. It has completely tanked my productivity at work, and I've missed critical family events and stopped going to the salsa dancing and social meetups I used to love.\n\nDoctor: Have these checking rituals and intrusive thoughts been present for at least 2 weeks, and do you feel they are excessive or unreasonable even while you can’t stop them?\n\nPatient: Yes, this has been building up for way longer than two weeks—it actually started about two years ago after my promotion at work. I absolutely know that these thoughts and checking habits are completely irrational and unreasonable, but despite knowing that, I still feel totally powerless to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: When you try to resist the checking or mental reviewing, do you feel intense anxiety or distress that only goes down after you perform the ritual?\n\nPatient: Yes, resisting it is almost impossible because the anxiety becomes absolutely crushing, and I get this terrifying feeling that something catastrophic is about to happen. The only way that intense panic finally fades is if I give in and complete the checking ritual until it feels \"just right.\"\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other repetitive behaviors or mental rituals besides checking, such as counting, ordering, repeating words silently, or needing things to feel exactly 'just right'?\n\nPatient: I don't do things like counting, ordering, or repeating words, but I absolutely need things to feel \"just right.\" When I'm checking a configuration or jiggling a door handle, I have to repeat the physical ritual until it feels exactly right, otherwise I'm overwhelmed by the feeling that something catastrophic will happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you recognize these thoughts and checking rituals as your own thoughts that feel unwanted or unreasonable, or do you ever believe they are actually true or that someone/something outside you is controlling them?\n\nPatient: I absolutely recognize them as my own thoughts, and I know they are completely irrational and unreasonable. Nobody else is controlling my mind, and I don't believe these fears are literally true, but the anxiety is so intense that I still feel forced to do the rituals anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to do the checking even when you are not anxious or worried, or is it mainly to reduce the intrusive fear that something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: No, I never do it when I'm feeling calm or unbothered. It is entirely driven by that intense, intrusive fear that something catastrophic is going to happen and it will be my fault. I only do the checking to try and shake that crushing feeling of worry so I can get some relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have any other obsessive-compulsive symptoms, such as contamination fears and washing, ordering/symmetry rituals, counting, or repeating words or prayers in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience any of those things like contamination fears, washing, counting, or repeating words. My struggles are strictly focused on my checking routines for work configurations and door locks to prevent something catastrophic from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do these checking rituals or intrusive fears ever feel like they are not really yours, or do you ever hear/feel a voice or outside force telling you to do them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't hear any voices or feel like an outside force is controlling me. Even though these thoughts and urges feel like they ambush me out of nowhere, I recognize them as coming entirely from my own mind. They are just incredibly intrusive and hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: When you try to resist the checking, do you feel a strong urge to repeat it until it feels exactly right or until the anxiety drops, even if you already know the task is done?\n\nPatient: Yes, that is exactly what happens. If I try to resist, this crushing anxiety builds up and forces me to keep rereading the configuration lines or jiggling the door handle. I have to repeat it over and over until it feels \"just right\" and the dread subsides, even though intellectually I know it was already done correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that the checking thoughts or urges are actually reasonable or true, or do they always feel unwanted and irrational to you even when they are hard to resist?"

Nobody else is controlling my mind, and I don't believe these fears are literally true, but the anxiety is so intense that I still feel forced to do the rituals anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to do the checking even when you are not anxious or worried, or is it mainly to reduce the intrusive fear that something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: No, I never do it when I'm feeling calm or unbothered. It is entirely driven by that intense, intrusive fear that something catastrophic is going to happen and it will be my fault. I only do the checking to try and shake that crushing feeling of worry so I can get some relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have any other obsessive-compulsive symptoms, such as contamination fears and washing, ordering/symmetry rituals, counting, or repeating words or prayers in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience any of those things like contamination fears, washing, counting, or repeating words. My struggles are strictly focused on my checking routines for work configurations and door locks to prevent something catastrophic from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do these checking rituals or intrusive fears ever feel like they are not really yours, or do you ever hear/feel a voice or outside force telling you to do them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't hear any voices or feel like an outside force is controlling me. Even though these thoughts and urges feel like they ambush me out of nowhere, I recognize them as coming entirely from my own mind. They are just incredibly intrusive and hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: When you try to resist the checking, do you feel a strong urge to repeat it until it feels exactly right or until the anxiety drops, even if you already know the task is done?\n\nPatient: Yes, that is exactly what happens. If I try to resist, this crushing anxiety builds up and forces me to keep rereading the configuration lines or jiggling the door handle. I have to repeat it over and over until it feels \"just right\" and the dread subsides, even though intellectually I know it was already done correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that the checking thoughts or urges are actually reasonable or true, or do they always feel unwanted and irrational to you even when they are hard to resist?\n\nPatient: They always feel completely unwanted and irrational to me, and I know the work is already done right. But even when I tell myself that, the overwhelming \"what if\" anxiety and the terrifying fear of a catastrophe just take over. That dread makes it feel absolutely impossible to resist the urge to check, no matter how unreasonable I know it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend more time on the intrusive thoughts, more time on the checking rituals, or are both about equally severe?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
They are both incredibly severe because they feed into each other in a constant, exhausting cycle. The intrusive thoughts and vivid "what-if" flashes are always running in the background, while the physical checking rituals—like rereading code or jiggling door handles—are what physically consume hours of my day. It really feels like the thoughts are constantly driving the rituals, making them both equally debilitating.

I only do the checking to try and shake that crushing feeling of worry so I can get some relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have any other obsessive-compulsive symptoms, such as contamination fears and washing, ordering/symmetry rituals, counting, or repeating words or prayers in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience any of those things like contamination fears, washing, counting, or repeating words. My struggles are strictly focused on my checking routines for work configurations and door locks to prevent something catastrophic from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do these checking rituals or intrusive fears ever feel like they are not really yours, or do you ever hear/feel a voice or outside force telling you to do them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't hear any voices or feel like an outside force is controlling me. Even though these thoughts and urges feel like they ambush me out of nowhere, I recognize them as coming entirely from my own mind. They are just incredibly intrusive and hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: When you try to resist the checking, do you feel a strong urge to repeat it until it feels exactly right or until the anxiety drops, even if you already know the task is done?\n\nPatient: Yes, that is exactly what happens. If I try to resist, this crushing anxiety builds up and forces me to keep rereading the configuration lines or jiggling the door handle. I have to repeat it over and over until it feels \"just right\" and the dread subsides, even though intellectually I know it was already done correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that the checking thoughts or urges are actually reasonable or true, or do they always feel unwanted and irrational to you even when they are hard to resist?\n\nPatient: They always feel completely unwanted and irrational to me, and I know the work is already done right. But even when I tell myself that, the overwhelming \"what if\" anxiety and the terrifying fear of a catastrophe just take over. That dread makes it feel absolutely impossible to resist the urge to check, no matter how unreasonable I know it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend more time on the intrusive thoughts, more time on the checking rituals, or are both about equally severe?\n\nPatient: They are both incredibly severe because they feed into each other in a constant, exhausting cycle. The intrusive thoughts and vivid \"what-if\" flashes are always running in the background, while the physical checking rituals—like rereading code or jiggling door handles—are what physically consume hours of my day. It really feels like the thoughts are constantly driving the rituals, making them both equally debilitating.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other OCD symptoms, such as contamination fears with washing, symmetry/ordering rituals, counting, or repeating words or prayers in your mind?"
  }

My current struggles are strictly focused on checking and re-checking to prevent catastrophic mistakes or harm.

I only do the checking to try and shake that crushing feeling of worry so I can get some relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have any other obsessive-compulsive symptoms, such as contamination fears and washing, ordering/symmetry rituals, counting, or repeating words or prayers in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience any of those things like contamination fears, washing, counting, or repeating words. My struggles are strictly focused on my checking routines for work configurations and door locks to prevent something catastrophic from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do these checking rituals or intrusive fears ever feel like they are not really yours, or do you ever hear/feel a voice or outside force telling you to do them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't hear any voices or feel like an outside force is controlling me. Even though these thoughts and urges feel like they ambush me out of nowhere, I recognize them as coming entirely from my own mind. They are just incredibly intrusive and hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: When you try to resist the checking, do you feel a strong urge to repeat it until it feels exactly right or until the anxiety drops, even if you already know the task is done?\n\nPatient: Yes, that is exactly what happens. If I try to resist, this crushing anxiety builds up and forces me to keep rereading the configuration lines or jiggling the door handle. I have to repeat it over and over until it feels \"just right\" and the dread subsides, even though intellectually I know it was already done correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that the checking thoughts or urges are actually reasonable or true, or do they always feel unwanted and irrational to you even when they are hard to resist?\n\nPatient: They always feel completely unwanted and irrational to me, and I know the work is already done right. But even when I tell myself that, the overwhelming \"what if\" anxiety and the terrifying fear of a catastrophe just take over. That dread makes it feel absolutely impossible to resist the urge to check, no matter how unreasonable I know it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend more time on the intrusive thoughts, more time on the checking rituals, or are both about equally severe?\n\nPatient: They are both incredibly severe because they feed into each other in a constant, exhausting cycle. The intrusive thoughts and vivid \"what-if\" flashes are always running in the background, while the physical checking rituals—like rereading code or jiggling door handles—are what physically consume hours of my day. It really feels like the thoughts are constantly driving the rituals, making them both equally debilitating.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other OCD symptoms, such as contamination fears with washing, symmetry/ordering rituals, counting, or repeating words or prayers in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any contamination fears, washing, counting, or praying rituals. While I've always been a highly organized person who loves keeping detailed spreadsheets and organizing my vinyl collection, those habits never felt like distressing rituals. My current struggles are strictly focused on checking and re-checking to prevent catastrophic mistakes or harm.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears and urges, do you ever believe they are definitely true or that the danger is really happening, or do you always recognize them as excessive and unreasonable even though they feel hard to resist?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Intellectually, I always recognize that these fears are irrational and that I've already done the work or locked the door correctly. However, in the moment, the overwhelming anxiety and that persistent "what if" feeling make the danger feel so real. Despite my logical understanding, the urge to check still feels absolutely impossible to resist.

The only way that intense panic finally fades is if I give in and complete the checking ritual until it feels \"just right.\"\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other repetitive behaviors or mental rituals besides checking, such as counting, ordering, repeating words silently, or needing things to feel exactly 'just right'?\n\nPatient: I don't do things like counting, ordering, or repeating words, but I absolutely need things to feel \"just right.\" When I'm checking a configuration or jiggling a door handle, I have to repeat the physical ritual until it feels exactly right, otherwise I'm overwhelmed by the feeling that something catastrophic will happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you recognize these thoughts and checking rituals as your own thoughts that feel unwanted or unreasonable, or do you ever believe they are actually true or that someone/something outside you is controlling them?\n\nPatient: I absolutely recognize them as my own thoughts, and I know they are completely irrational and unreasonable. Nobody else is controlling my mind, and I don't believe these fears are literally true, but the anxiety is so intense that I still feel forced to do the rituals anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to do the checking even when you are not anxious or worried, or is it mainly to reduce the intrusive fear that something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: No, I never do it when I'm feeling calm or unbothered. It is entirely driven by that intense, intrusive fear that something catastrophic is going to happen and it will be my fault. I only do the checking to try and shake that crushing feeling of worry so I can get some relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have any other obsessive-compulsive symptoms, such as contamination fears and washing, ordering/symmetry rituals, counting, or repeating words or prayers in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience any of those things like contamination fears, washing, counting, or repeating words. My struggles are strictly focused on my checking routines for work configurations and door locks to prevent something catastrophic from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do these checking rituals or intrusive fears ever feel like they are not really yours, or do you ever hear/feel a voice or outside force telling you to do them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't hear any voices or feel like an outside force is controlling me. Even though these thoughts and urges feel like they ambush me out of nowhere, I recognize them as coming entirely from my own mind. They are just incredibly intrusive and hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: When you try to resist the checking, do you feel a strong urge to repeat it until it feels exactly right or until the anxiety drops, even if you already know the task is done?\n\nPatient: Yes, that is exactly what happens. If I try to resist, this crushing anxiety builds up and forces me to keep rereading the configuration lines or jiggling the door handle. I have to repeat it over and over until it feels \"just right\" and the dread subsides, even though intellectually I know it was already done correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that the checking thoughts or urges are actually reasonable or true, or do they always feel unwanted and irrational to you even when they are hard to resist?\n\nPatient: They always feel completely unwanted and irrational to me, and I know the work is already done right. But even when I tell myself that, the overwhelming \"what if\" anxiety and the terrifying fear of a catastrophe just take over. That dread makes it feel absolutely impossible to resist the urge to check, no matter how unreasonable I know it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend more time on the intrusive thoughts, more time on the checking rituals, or are both about equally severe?\n\nPatient: They are both incredibly severe because they feed into each other in a constant, exhausting cycle. The intrusive thoughts and vivid \"what-if\" flashes are always running in the background, while the physical checking rituals—like rereading code or jiggling door handles—are what physically consume hours of my day. It really feels like the thoughts are constantly driving the rituals, making them both equally debilitating.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other OCD symptoms, such as contamination fears with washing, symmetry/ordering rituals, counting, or repeating words or prayers in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any contamination fears, washing, counting, or praying rituals. While I've always been a highly organized person who loves keeping detailed spreadsheets and organizing my vinyl collection, those habits never felt like distressing rituals. My current struggles are strictly focused on checking and re-checking to prevent catastrophic mistakes or harm.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears and urges, do you ever believe they are definitely true or that the danger is really happening, or do you always recognize them as excessive and unreasonable even though they feel hard to resist?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I always recognize that these fears are irrational and that I've already done the work or locked the door correctly. However, in the moment, the overwhelming anxiety and that persistent \"what if\" feeling make the danger feel so real. Despite my logical understanding, the urge to check still feels absolutely impossible to resist.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have any other obsessive-compulsive symptoms, such as contamination fears with washing, symmetry or ordering rituals, counting, or repeating words or prayers in your mind?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't have any of those symptoms like contamination fears, washing, counting, or symmetry rituals. My struggles are strictly focused on my checking behaviors, like repeatedly reviewing my work configs or jiggling door locks, because of the fear of making a catastrophic mistake.

Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.
